The experiments assess the feasibility of this combinatorial-algebraic approach and provide… ▽ More We investigate an algebraic approach to the Syndrome Decoding Problem, based on a reformulation of the Hamming weight constraint and its integration with the Information Set Decoding paradigm. We begin with a systematic analysis of the Hamming variety, deriving its defining equations in terms of elementary symmetric functions. Since these equations may have high degree, we exploit convolution identities for elementary symmetric functions, together with factorizations based on Lucas' identity, to derive an equivalent formulation with auxiliary variables and equations of bounded degree. Building on this modeling, we generalize the ISD paradigm through an ISD-like decoding strategy, implemented by the GBDecode algorithm, in which only a subset of an information set is fixed. This approach reduces the size of the combinatorial search space at the cost of solving the associated multivariate nonlinear systems. To handle this algebraic component, we employ the MultiSolve algorithm, which replaces a single Grobner basis computation with a collection of computations on simpler systems, obtained by exhaustively assigning a varying number of indeterminates over the finite field. This provides a tunable balance between combinatorial search and algebraic solving. We evaluate the resulting approach experimentally on instances of the Syndrome Decoding Problem for random binary linear codes, using parameters corresponding to the NIST Security Category 1 parameter set of the Classic McEliece cryptosystem. The experiments assess the feasibility of this combinatorial-algebraic approach and provide insights into the practical behavior of Grobner basis techniques within an ISD-like decoding framework. △ Less

    Fractal dimension of singular times for SPDEs: Energy bounds, criticality, and weak-strong uniqueness

    Authors: Antonio Agresti

    Abstract: …time integrability and the excess of spatial regularity compared to the critical regularity of the energy bound associated with weak solutions, respectively. Moreover, their corresponding $(1-\ell\,\mathsf{Exc} )$-dimensional measure is zero. We formulate and apply our theory to annealed strong Leray-Hopf solutions of 3D Navier-Stokes equations (NSEs) with p… ▽ More For several physically relevant SPDEs, it is known that global weak solutions coexist with local strong ones. Typically, weak-strong uniqueness results are known, and ensure that the global and strong solutions coincide as long as the latter exist. Times at which a weak solution does not coincide with a strong one are called singular times. Determining their fractal dimension is fundamental to capturing the regularity of weak solutions. We introduce a probabilistic notion of singular times for a wide class of semilinear SPDEs. We show that sets of singular times have fractal dimension (i.e., Hausdorff and/or Minkowski) at most $ 1-\ell\, \mathsf{Exc}$, where $\ell$ and $\mathsf{Exc}$ are the time integrability and the excess of spatial regularity compared to the critical regularity of the energy bound associated with weak solutions, respectively. Moreover, their corresponding $(1-\ell\,\mathsf{Exc} )$-dimensional measure is zero. We formulate and apply our theory to annealed strong Leray-Hopf solutions of 3D Navier-Stokes equations (NSEs) with physically relevant noises, including rough Kraichnan and Lie transport. In particular, we extend the fundamental $1/2$-dimensional bound of Leray and Scheffer on singular times for 3D NSEs to the stochastic setting, and we prove new conditional results under supercritical Serrin's conditions, irrespective of the roughness of the noise. Our framework is new even in the deterministic case, and provides the first partial regularity results for weak solutions to SPDEs with multiplicative noise. △ Less

    Authors: Svante Janson, Tiffany Y. Y. Lo, Colin McDiarmid, Anda Skeja, Fiona Skerman

We develop the representation, equivalence and convergence t… ▽ More We develop a framework for pure and mixed graphon colourings and use it to study the limiting behaviour of graph partition parameters under cut convergence. Pure colourings correspond to measurable partitions, while mixed colourings assign a probability distribution on the colour set to each point. We develop the representation, equivalence and convergence theory, and show that every mixed graphon colouring has an equivalent pure representative, possibly on a different probability space. For each fixed finite colour set, we establish compactness and describe the topology of coloured graph limits using the cut norm on graphons and the weak-$*$ topology on colourings. The usual coloured cut distance characterises convergence for pure colourings, but not for general mixed colourings. As applications, we prove that the Cheeger constant and modularity converge to their mixed graphon counterparts along cut-convergent graph sequences whose orders tend to infinity, assuming uniformly linear minimum degree and positive limiting edge density, respectively. Both mixed functionals are invariant under graphon equivalence. We also establish their continuity in cut distance on the corresponding classes of graphons, with a stability bound for the Cheeger constant. For modularity, we further characterise coloured graphons attaining extremal modularity values and prove attainment of the mixed optimum for each fixed number of colours. We also exhibit a graphon whose modularity supremum is not attained by any finite colouring. △ Less

    Authors: Hanyang Qian, Wen Sun, Juntao Huo, Guowei Li

    Abstract: …the large differential response is tied to field-induced spin reorientation. Thermal expansion also changes across the spin-reorientation boundary. The combined results support a mechanism in which competing magne… ▽ More Achieving a large strain response to a small magnetic-field variation remains a challenge in magnetomechanical materials, particularly in topological systems where the coupling between spin reconstruction and lattice deformation remains unclear. Here, we report an anomalous magnetostrictive response associated with field-induced spin reorientation in the topological Kagome magnet TbMn6Sn6. Below its spin-reorientation temperature of 310 K, an in-plane magnetic field induces an abrupt increase in magnetostriction, which exceeds 120 ppm at 240 K. This transition produces a giant differential piezomagnetic coefficient of approximately 5000 ppm T-1, far exceeding the values reported for other topological magnetic materials and commercial FeGa alloys, and placing TbMn6Sn6 among the most field-sensitive magnetostrictive materials. The field position of this peak agrees with the anomalies in magnetization and magnetotransport, demonstrating that the large differential response is tied to field-induced spin reorientation. Thermal expansion also changes across the spin-reorientation boundary. Raman measurements reveal corresponding anomalies in phonon energy and linewidth, indicating that the reorientation modifies the lattice dynamics. The combined results support a mechanism in which competing magnetic anisotropies of the Tb and Mn sublattices, coupled through Tb-Mn exchange, create magnetic configurations with different equilibrium strains. This finding establishes spin reorientation as a route to giant piezomagnetic responses in topological magnets and provides a basis for magnetomechanical sensing and switching near room temperature. △ Less

    Authors: Abhradipta Ghosh, Vaibhav Sherkar, Bilol Banerjee, Anil K. Ghosh

These $L1$ distance based tes… ▽ More In recent years, optimal transport has emerged as a useful framework for constructing distribution-free multivariate two-sample tests by transporting observations to a reference distribution under a suitable transportation cost. However, existing optimal transport-based tests can perform poorly in high dimension, low sample size (HDLSS) settings, and their performance depends critically on the choice of the reference distribution and transportation cost. We investigate this phenomenon and show that suitable choices can substantially improve their performance. Motivated by this analysis, we introduce a class of two-sample tests based on optimal derangements that do not require an external reference distribution. The minimum distance pairing test of Rosenbaum arises as a special case when the optimization is restricted to a subclass of derangements, thereby revealing a connection between this method and optimal transport. We propose the optimal derangement (ODER) test and its distribution-free variant, the ODER-SHC test, obtained by restricting the minimization to derangements consisting of a single Hamiltonian cycle. We establish their consistency under both HDLSS and classical large-sample asymptotic regimes. We also develop corresponding procedures based on the $L1$ distance and establish their high-dimensional consistency under conditions involving coordinate-wise energy distances. These $L1$ distance based tests can also discriminate between two high-dimensional distributions with identical first two moments but different marginal distributions. Analyses of benchmark datasets and extensive simulation studies demonstrate the performance of the proposed methods across a variety of alternatives and data settings. △ Less

    Leaving the Cave by Breaking the Symmetry: Identifiable Structure Can Enable Cross-Lingual Generalization

    Authors: Frederick Riemenschneider, Anette Frank

    Abstract: …multilingual model, we propose the Relational Identifiability Hypothesis: relations in a shared world can promote entity alignment by ruling out alternative cross-lingual correspondences, making… ▽ More Why do multilingual language models generalize across languages even without shared vocabulary? The Platonic Representation Hypothesis posits that models trained on different data, objectives, and modalities converge toward a shared statistical model of reality. Extending this intuition to representations inside a single multilingual model, we propose the Relational Identifiability Hypothesis: relations in a shared world can promote entity alignment by ruling out alternative cross-lingual correspondences, making corresponding entities more likely to become directly readable without an additional transformation. We test this hypothesis in a controlled bilingual Transformer whose two token-disjoint languages describe the same modular-arithmetic world. We find that the languages can develop a shared answer subspace without aligning corresponding numbers. Cross-lingual alignment emerges selectively when the available relations constrain number correspondences: subtraction makes zero task-level identifiable, inducing selective alignment, while an order-sensitive operation produces broad alignment across numbers. These patterns predict success in zero-shot cross-lingual transfer. Together, our results show that relational structure in a shared world can promote entity alignment and enable transfer. △ Less

Although transverse Seebeck effect has been investigated in various materials, its reciprocal process, transverse Peltier effect, remains much less explored, and direct comparison of the two effects in the same homogeneous material is still limited. Here, we experimentally investigate both transverse Seebeck and Peltier effects in a single crystal of MoSi2, an axis-dependent conduction polarity material in which the sign of the Seebeck coefficient varies depending on the crystallographic axis. Multi-terminal voltage measurement determines the direction of the induced internal electric field and establishes the transverse Seebeck response. These results establish reciprocal transverse thermoelectric conversion in a homogeneous nonmagnetic single crystal without engineered interfaces. △ Less

    Authors: Partha Ghose

    Abstract: …. Spatial separation of the E and I complexes need not preclude coherent superposition: when they constitute distinct subsystems, the corresponding collective alternatives can form an entangled state. The proposal tests temporal correlations, not Bell nonlocality. Complementary feedforward detectors couple selectively to E or I, with ideal negative results e… ▽ More Recent efforts to identify quantum-like signatures in brain and cognition have explored Bell-type tests, temporal inequalities, and Schrödinger-inspired models. We propose a Leggett--Garg (LG) test of collective neuronal excitation--inhibition (E--I) dynamics. Two reference configurations, $\ket{E}$ and $\ket{I}$, span an effective two-state space governed by $H_S=\heffΩσ_x/2$. Spatial separation of the E and I complexes need not preclude coherent superposition: when they constitute distinct subsystems, the corresponding collective alternatives can form an entangled state. The proposal tests temporal correlations, not Bell nonlocality. Complementary feedforward detectors couple selectively to E or I, with ideal negative results enabling non-invasive inference of the opposite configuration. We give a four-output-cell realization of the required projectors and detector interactions. Ideal sequential measurements yield $K_3(τ)=2\cos(Ωτ)-\cos(2Ωτ)$, reaching $3/2$. We also derive the four-time result $K_4^{\max}=2\sqrt{2}$ and explain its structural CHSH correspondence through Choi--Jamiołkowski duality. Detector efficiency, false positives, imperfect null results, timing uncertainty, and measurement disturbance are incorporated into a realistic extension. We conclude by identifying the experimental challenges of preparing, controlling, and selectively measuring collective E--I states in neuronal circuits. △ Less

    Authors: Yves Dumont, Marc Hétier, Valaire Yatat-Djeumen

    Abstract: …a generalist, whilst the other is sedentary and lives in the forest), and blood feeding preferences between multiple blood sources. A comprehensive analysis of the system is carried out using classical theory… ▽ More Tropical forests are undergoing deep changes caused by human activities, which have repercussions on the habitats of wild animal, particularly mammals. As mammals serve as a main source of blood meals for forest mosquitoes, their decline may affect the mosquitoes, as do changes in their habitats and abiotic conditions, such as rainfall, temperature, etc. Finally, along with humans come new species of mosquitoes-more generalist and more anthropophilic-that adapt to these new conditions and, moreover, serve as vectors for re-emerging diseases against which local populations have no more immunity. In this work, we derive a deterministic model which includes the effects of anthropisation, two species of mosquito that interact with one another (one is a generalist, whilst the other is sedentary and lives in the forest), and blood feeding preferences between multiple blood sources. We consider two socio-ecological areas, corresponding to a domestic area (for instance, a village), and a wild area (for instance, a tropical forest). A comprehensive analysis of the system is carried out using classical theory and monotone systems theory. We analyse the efficiency of individual protective measures, such as mosquito nets and repellents, against the domestic mosquito species. We investigate the risk of invasion of the wild area by a generalist mosquito species. We show that habitat anthropisation and high interspecific competition facilitate the invasion. When the mathematical system converges towards a periodic cycle, we provide numerical estimates for the periodic invasion threshold. Using a global sensitivity analysis, we show that the model is sensitive to the level of anthropisation, the adaptability of the invasive species to the forest area, and its fitness. The more efficient way to control the invasion is, therefore, to limit, as much as possible, the forest anthropisation. △ Less

    Beyond Reachability: When Edit Order Preserves Couplings in Graph Flow Matching

    Authors: Zilin Li, Zixing Song

    Abstract: Shortest-path optimality does not guarantee that a memoryless graph generator preserves a prescribed source-target correspondence. We study whether reordering legal shortest edits can retain this correspondence without changing the pairing, mandatory edits, or sampling budget. Under a common Poisson clock, we establish… ▽ More Shortest-path optimality does not guarantee that a memoryless graph generator preserves a prescribed source-target correspondence. We study whether reordering legal shortest edits can retain this correspondence without changing the pairing, mandatory edits, or sampling budget. Under a common Poisson clock, we establish an exact criterion for endpoint coupling recoverability: the source-blind Markov projection attains the unavoidable clock-error floor if and only if routes to distinct targets do not share graph states. Routes to the same target may merge without sacrificing endpoint fidelity. This characterization leads to an exact finite-state routing formulation and a legality-preserving scheduling procedure with recovery certificates. A marker-free construction demonstrates how shared edge structure can retain source distinctions, while a capacity-constrained family exhibits a sharp separation in paired error between edit orders with identical marginal error. Finally, a target-conditioned aliasing bound connects structural route design to the terminal fidelity of a learned graph-only generator. These results characterize when legal edit ordering can preserve a prescribed coupling under fixed operational costs. △ Less

Recent trajectory-based methods leverage long-term point tracks for explicit temporal correspondence, but often treat trajectories as carriers of sampled visual features or summarize their motion with compact 2D displacement-based descriptors. We instead view an action as the ordered evolution of 2.5D trajectory states and their local geometric interactions. Based on this view, we introduce GeoTRAM, a geometry-aware trajectory state modeling framework for FSAR. GeoTRAM lifts sparse tracks into 2.5D trajectories, where StateMotion models ordered intra-trajectory state evolution and RelMotion captures state-conditioned local interactions through time-varying relative geometry and motion. Quality-filtered spectral sampling provides more reliable trajectory initialization; complementary semantic, motion, and depth cues are separately refined and preserved for joint spatio-temporal modeling; action-guided reweighting and top-$ρ$ trajectory selection support complementary frame- and trajectory-level matching.     GPA-VGGT: Geometry-Preserving Acceleration of VGGT

    Authors: Muyeol Sung, Jangwon Kim, Jongwoo Lim

    Abstract: …queries, restore the attention mass of the tokens each merged key represents, and randomly project the matching metric to 128 dimensions to reduce matching cost. Across diverse multi-view geometry tasks, GPA-VGGT provides training-free acceleration at speedups comparable to prior… ▽ More Feed-forward geometry models such as VGGT recover camera parameters, depth maps, and point maps from one to hundreds of views in a single forward pass, making them powerful backbones for multi-view 3D perception. However, VGGT's global attention is quadratic in the number of frames and dominates end-to-end runtime on long sequences, which has motivated a range of training-free efficiency methods such as token merging. These methods, however, come with a drop in accuracy that makes them hard to use in place of VGGT. We propose GPA-VGGT, a training-free acceleration method that replaces global attention in early blocks with frame attention augmented by a shared cross-frame K/V set. In the remaining blocks, we merge only keys and values while preserving all queries, restore the attention mass of the tokens each merged key represents, and randomly project the matching metric to 128 dimensions to reduce matching cost. Across diverse multi-view geometry tasks, GPA-VGGT provides training-free acceleration at speedups comparable to prior work while preserving the information required for precise geometric estimation, retaining over 99% of VGGT's pose accuracy under AUC@3. △ Less

    Benchmarking Universal Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials for Temperature-Dependent Elasticity of Binary and High-Entropy Refractory Carbides

    Authors: Miroslav Lebeda, Jan Drahokoupil, Šimon Svoboda, Petr Vlčák

    Abstract: …Here, we benchmark nine uMLIPs against consistent AIMD reference data for five binary and two high-entropy (HE) carbides between 300 and 1200 K. Elastic constants and the corresponding bulk, shear, and Young's moduli are obtained using stress-strain molecular dynamics, explicitly sampling thermal atomic motion and anharmonic effects beyond thermal expan… ▽ More Accurate predictions of temperature-dependent stiffness are essential for assessing the high-temperature performance of refractory carbides, yet the cost of ab initio molecular dynamics (AIMD) limits systematic exploration across compositions and temperatures. Universal machine-learning interatomic potentials (uMLIPs) offer a computationally efficient alternative, but their reliability for finite-temperature elasticity remains insufficiently established. Here, we benchmark nine uMLIPs against consistent AIMD reference data for five binary and two high-entropy (HE) carbides between 300 and 1200 K. Elastic constants and the corresponding bulk, shear, and Young's moduli are obtained using stress-strain molecular dynamics, explicitly sampling thermal atomic motion and anharmonic effects beyond thermal expansion alone. We assess absolute elastic properties and normalized thermal softening separately. MACE-MH-1 achieves the lowest overall mean absolute percentage error (5.7%). MACE-MH-1 and DPA4-Mini reproduce thermal softening most accurately, with mean deviations of 2.5 and 2.3 percentage points, respectively. All models generally underestimate stiffness, with larger equilibrium volumes relative to the AIMD reference likely contributing to this trend for most models. C12 exhibits the largest model-dependent errors. The HE carbides are described with accuracy comparable to that of the binary carbides, indicating no apparent accuracy penalty from chemical complexity. Accuracy instead varies with transition-metal composition, with group-V carbides, particularly TaC, presenting the greatest challenge. These results identify promising pretrained models for finite-temperature elasticity in carbides and show why accurate absolute stiffness and thermal softening must be assessed independently. △ Less

    Abstract: …overlapping continuation places generated history at the prescribed sampling noise level and commits only newly generated latents. Together, these mechanisms ensure exact preser… ▽ More Continuous visual world simulation requires coupling evolving scene geometry with temporally persistent appearance. We present UniWorld, a unified framework that integrates depth trajectories and reference appearance within a shared video generation model. The central design maintains visual and geometric histories on a common timeline. Noise-aligned overlapping continuation places generated history at the prescribed sampling noise level and commits only newly generated latents. Rolling depth latent state preserves the corresponding geometric controls, incrementally reads newly available geometry, and keeps the conditioning fixed within each sampling window. Together, these mechanisms ensure exact preservation of latent history while bounding the active depth state by the window size. We evaluate continuous generation, paired history interventions, and long-horizon behavior across diverse scene categories. The same generator supports estimated, native, and on-demand simulator depth, while cached and incremental execution paths produce bitwise-identical outputs. Appearance changes and geometric control branches operate through the same interface without modifying committed history. The measured steady-state DiT execution path achieves real-time generation throughput, demonstrating the efficiency of UniWorld for continuous visual world simulation.     Authors: V. Tsiantou, V. Koukouloyannis, K. D. Housiadas, P. G. Kevrekidis

    Abstract: …improved approximations of the stability eigenvalues, with particular attention to the unstable real eigenvalue of the in-phase configuration. Power-series approximations are derived for three representative configurations: a single-site soliton and the two two-site ones, namely the in-phase and the out-of-phase configurations. Although higher-order truncations are increasingly accurate for small $\varepsilon$, the expansions possess a small radius of convergence and cease to be useful well before $\varepsilon={\cal O}(1)$. To extend their range of validity we apply the Euler convergence-acceleration method, constructing an optimally transformed series for each lattice site individually. The transformed solution is shown to have substantially better convergence radius and preserves the localization of the solution much better than the original series solution. We then insert the series profiles into the linearization matrix of the DNLS equation in order to obtain improved approximations of the stability eigenvalues, with particular attention to the unstable real eigenvalue of the in-phase configuration. Optimal truncation of the corresponding expansion yields a closed-form ${\cal O}(\varepsilon^{5/2})$ estimate, which significantly extends the known ${\cal O}(\varepsilon^{1/2})$ leading-order result to considerably larger couplings, while the semi-numerical use of the accelerated profiles reproduces the numerically computed eigenvalue to values of $\varepsilon$ of ${\cal O}(1)$. Throughout, both the solution profiles and their stability eigenvalues are benchmarked against the numerical solution obtained through a continuation procedure from the anti-continuum limit. △ Less

    Authors: Hugo Math, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Christos Anagnostopoulos

    Abstract: …event sequences (DESs), including operational event logs or patient clinical-event trajectories, have domain-specific event vocabularies whose labels lack a consistent correspondence across domains. Beyond this lack of consistency, the number of distinct events in real-world settings is high enough to challenge both classical temporal point process modeling… ▽ More Temporal foundation models such as Chronos and TimesFM generalize across domains through a shared numerical observation space. In contrast, discrete event sequences (DESs), including operational event logs or patient clinical-event trajectories, have domain-specific event vocabularies whose labels lack a consistent correspondence across domains. Beyond this lack of consistency, the number of distinct events in real-world settings is high enough to challenge both classical temporal point process modeling and current in-context foundation inference models such as FIM-PP. To address this, we propose two complementary modules: (i) DES-JEPA, a joint-embedding predictive architecture that learns a shared discrete vocabulary from synthetic stochastic processes with unaligned event vocabularies; and (ii) DES-PFN, a discrete event sequence prior-fitted network that reuses the learned vocabulary for in-context next-event prediction. Gray blockouts, colored primitives, semantic maps, and wireframes all work as input. It also preserves entity correspondence: group counts stay correct, and entities appear and disappear on the right frame. We implement AnyGameRenderer by LoRA fine-tuning MiniMax-H3 ref2va, a 33B reference-video-conditioned video generation model. Training uses ~10k paired clips from three sources: (i) multi-stream rendering data from 38 agent-made games; (ii) two-stream rendering data from Unreal Engine, with programmatically controlled crowds, count changes, and entity scales; and (iii) real gameplay recordings, with pairs reconstructed via 3D-box detection. To evaluate the entity correspondence, we propose an entity-level benchmark of 59 held-out cases with 753 entities, scored against ground-truth boxes. Compared with the base model, AnyGameRenderer better preserves entity correspondence: temporal match rises from 50.9% to 62.9%, missed count changes fall from 34.7% to 21.3%, and fewer entities are hallucinated or left unrendered. Project page: https://alaya-lab.github.io/AnyGameRenderer. △ Less

Progress in this area is hindered by two key limitations: (1) the lack of well-defined interleaved generation tasks and corresponding training data, and (2) the absence of fine-grained evaluation protocols that can reliably diagnose model performance across different capability dimensions. Extensive experiments validate our evaluation suite through low inter-metric redundancy (max pairwise absolute Spearman's $ρ\leq 0.42$ ), high agreement with human judgments (83.3% accuracy), and strong consistency across different expert evaluators (Spearman's $ρ> 0.9$ ), supporting the complementarity and reliability of our metrics. By benchmarking four families of UMMs, we show that IntReal Evaluation Suite reveals distinct strengths and weaknesses across models. Post-training experiments demonstrate the utility of IntReal-100K for improving interleaved generation and further show that training unimodal generation can also benefit interleaved generation. These findings suggest that interleaved generation can benefit from modality-specific optimization, rather than requiring joint text-image training at every stage. △ Less

    Authors: Michał Kijaczko, Vivek Sahu

In the h… ▽ More We establish sharp fractional Hardy inequalities in the critical regimes $sp=d$ and $sp=1$, with explicit logarithmic weight functions. In the whole-space setting, for $p>d\ge 1$, $sp=d$, we prove that \begin{equation*} \int_{\mathbb R^d} \int_{\mathbb R^d} \frac{|u(x)-u(y)|^p}{|x-y|^{2d}}\,dx\,dy \geq \left(\frac{p-1}{p}\right)^p \int_{B_R}|u(x)|^p W_{p,d,R}(x)\,dx, \end{equation*} for $u\in C_c(B_R)$, where $W_{p,d,R}$ is a logarithmic-type weight and the constant is sharp. We also investigate the asymptotic behaviour of this weight, obtaining estimates that describe its behaviour near the boundary. These results elucidate the role of logarithmic weights in critical fractional Hardy inequalities in both whole-space and half-space settings. △ Less

    Using delays for prescribed-time static output feedback stabilization of LTI controllable and observable systems

    Authors: Kang-Kang Zhang, Emilia Fridman

    Abstract: …design is further extended to known bounded and fast-varying (without any constraints on the delay derivative) measurement delays, yielding fixed-time stabilization under a corresponding rank condition, with an assignable settling-time bound greater than three times the delay bound. For unknown constant measurement delays, fixed-time stabilization is establi… ▽ More This paper addresses a stabilization problem: can every controllable and observable LTI system be stabilized by static output feedback? To the best of our knowledge, we provide, for the first time, a solution to this problem. Furthermore, the proposed design achieves prescribed-time convergence to the origin, with the convergence time arbitrarily assigned a priori and independently of the initial condition. Two static output-feedback methods are developed. The first employs multiple artificial output delays with smooth time-varying gains and provides an explicit gain construction for every controllable and observable LTI system, based on a generalized controllability--observability Gramian introduced in this paper. The second combines constant-gain feedback of the current output with time-varying-gain feedback of a single artificially delayed output and also applies to every controllable and observable LTI system. Its simpler structure requires auxiliary parameters whose existence is guaranteed but whose explicit construction is not provided for general MIMO systems; for SISO systems, the constant current-output gain can be readily selected. The multiple-delay design is further extended to known bounded and fast-varying (without any constraints on the delay derivative) measurement delays, yielding fixed-time stabilization under a corresponding rank condition, with an assignable settling-time bound greater than three times the delay bound. For unknown constant measurement delays, fixed-time stabilization is established for systems with nilpotent state matrices. A numerical implementation is also presented. Numerical examples involving nonminimum-phase, flexible-structure, and cart--pole systems illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed methods. △ Less

Then, a two-layer optimization framework is developed with the outer and inner layers optimizing the bending curvature and the other two parameters, respectively. Numerical results based on the cascaded geometrical and wave channel model demonstrate that the proposed method achieves higher received signal power than conventional Airy-beam designs and offers complementary advantages over conventional focused beams under different blockage ratios. △ Less

    Partial Stabilizer Learning under Fixed Commuting Constraints and the Absence of a Copy-Rate Discount

    Authors: Masahito Hayashi, Yimin Lu

    Abstract: …with the prescribed observables, together with their eigenvalues. We compare the number \(k\) of copies available to the learner with the number \(n\) of qudits and refer to their ratio \(k/n\) as the copy rate.… ▽ More We consider an unknown pure stabilizer state of \(n\) qudits with a fixed prime local dimension \(p\), together with prescribed commuting Pauli observables. Rather than learning the whole state, the learner must recover only complementary stabilizers that commute with the prescribed observables, together with their eigenvalues. Once the prescribed measurement is performed, this information and the observed outcome determine the corresponding conditional stabilizer state. We compare the number \(k\) of copies available to the learner with the number \(n\) of qudits and refer to their ratio \(k/n\) as the copy rate. Suppose that the dimension \(m\) of the requested complementary stabilizer subspace satisfies \(m=βn+o(n)\) for a fixed \(0<β\leq1\). If the copy rate converges to a value below one, the optimal exact recovery probabilities and verification scores converge to zero, both in the worst case and under the uniform prior. Conversely, if the number \(k\) of copies minus the number \(n\) of qudits tends to positive infinity, the entire stabilizer state can be identified with probability tending to one, and the requested complementary information can then be extracted. Thus, restricting the learning target to the stabilizer information needed after the prescribed measurement yields no copy-rate discount. △ Less

When predicted character durations are quantized into frame counts at 12.5 Hz, durations shorter than one codec frame may be assigned zero frames. Consequently, the corresponding character representations may be lost during length regulation, potentially leading to pronunciation omissions. This study proposes DRC-TTS, a dual-resolution codec-based architecture for lexicon-free Malay speech synthesis. The model expands character representations at a temporal resolution of 50 Hz and subsequently downsamples them through strided convolution to 12.5 Hz for discrete codec-token prediction. Objective evaluations show that the proposed model reduces the character error rate (CER) from 27.11% for the baseline to 21.86%, an absolute reduction of 5.25 percentage points. The proposed model also achieves a mean opinion score (MOS) of 4.30, compared with 4.12 for the baseline. These results suggest that the dual-resolution design may improve character-level alignment and perceived speech naturalness under the evaluated conditions. △ Less

We develop a unified foundation for bisimulation metrics in undiscounted settings, that is total- and average-reward problems, by replacing the standard operator-centric view with a game-centric one: we interpret prior metric operators as Bellman-Shapley operators of zero-sum turn-based games and define metrics as the corresponding game value functions, yielding unique definitions even when $γ=1$. This game-theoretic foundation facilitates transfer of dynamic-programming theory and algorithms. We also use this game-centric perspective to characterize how metric finiteness depends on structural properties of the underlying MDP and motivate certain directed metrics that remain finite in weakly communicating MDPs. We further introduce an abstract framework for modifying the games' action spaces, allowing general transition coupling sets, that recovers many existing metrics and guides new constructions. Finally, we prove that these metrics bound total-reward and, when the optimal gain is state-independent, optimal relative value differences. We also provide error and suboptimality guarantees for aggregation-based planning in total- and average-reward settings. △ Less

    Spectral-Aligned Pruning for Universal Error-Correcting Code Transformers

    Authors: Sanghyeon Cho, Taewoo Park, Seong-Joon Park, Dae-Young Yun, Hee-Youl Kwak, Sang-Hyo Kim, Yongjune Kim

    Abstract: …for FECCT and propose Spectral-Aligned Pruning (SAP), a structure-aware framework that enables cross-code reuse of structured pruning masks by leveraging the spectrum of the corresponding bipartite graph. SAP is grounded in classical graph analysis of codes: the two algebraically largest adjacency eigenvalues provide compact spectral proxies for degree scale… ▽ More Universal channel decoders based on transformers-such as the Foundation Error Correction Code Transformer (FECCT)-achieve competitive decoding performance across diverse code families with a single shared backbone, optionally followed by code-specific finetuning. However, the high computational complexity and large parameter footprint of FECCT present substantial obstacles to practical deployment. To address these challenges, we investigate structured pruning for FECCT and propose Spectral-Aligned Pruning (SAP), a structure-aware framework that enables cross-code reuse of structured pruning masks by leveraging the spectrum of the corresponding bipartite graph. SAP is grounded in classical graph analysis of codes: the two algebraically largest adjacency eigenvalues provide compact spectral proxies for degree scale, expansion ratio, and minimum-distance lower bounds. These quantities are directly relevant to decoding performance: degree scale reflects how densely codeword bits and parity checks are connected; expansion ratio influences how information propagates across the bipartite graph; and minimum distance characterizes codeword separation. Based on this connection, SAP uses these two leading eigenvalues as a lightweight code signature for pruning-mask retrieval. Empirically, this two-dimensional signature yields stable library selection equivalent to higher-dimensional spectral signatures in our evaluation. After pruning, SAP uses parameter-efficient low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to recover decoding performance while storing only a small number of additional adapter parameters per code. Experiments across diverse codes show that SAP achieves decoding performance comparable to dedicated per-code pruning, while enabling substantial reductions in computational cost and model memory footprint through kernel-level structured pruning. Code is available at https://github.com/iil-postech/sap. △ Less

    Decoding cell signaling via optimal transport and information theory

    Authors: Mintu Nandi, Sosuke Ito

    Abstract: …intracellular signal relay than INF alone. Mutual information (MI) is widely used to quantify signaling fidelity, capturing how well outputs discriminate input states. However, it fails to capture whether the output preserves the statistical structure of the input, a property crucial in morphogen patterning and dose-dependent signaling. To address this gap, we introduce the 2-Wasserstein (2-WD) distance, which provides a geometric basis for comparing input and output distributions. We define MI as informational fidelity (INF) and the inverse of the 2-WD as geometric fidelity (GMF). Applying this dual-fidelity framework to canonical regulatory motifs under Gaussian channel approximation reveals topology-dependent trade-offs: coherent feed-forward loops can perform well in both dimensions, whereas feedback architectures reduce INF to enhance GMF. Experimental analysis of tumor necrosis factor signaling reveals dual-fidelity behavior qualitatively consistent with feedback regulation. RAS-MAPK data analysis further shows that jointly considering INF and GMF better characterizes intracellular signal relay than INF alone. Our results thus indicate that these signaling behaviors are not fully characterized by MI alone; instead, distributional correspondence provides a complementary dimension of signaling fidelity. Our study provides a practical framework for analyzing natural networks and guiding the design of task-specific synthetic circuits. △ Less

    A Witnessing Duality for ReLU Network Depth

    Authors: Dimitrios Myrisiotis

    Abstract: …for the network but not for the maximum, so one of finitely many test points exposes the error. By witnessing, provable refutation corresponds to a provably correct refuter, and provable existence of a certified network to a provably correct constructor. On the construction side, we introduce certificates that label each maximum gadget by the inputs it aggr… ▽ More A depth lower bound for ReLU networks asserts that every shallower network errs on some input; we ask when a weak theory can prove such a bound so that an error can be computed. For the exact computation of $\max(x_1,\ldots,x_n)$, recent work shows two hidden layers suffice with unrestricted rational weights. By contrast, Haase, Hertrich and Loho (ICLR 2023) proved that integer weights require depth logarithmic in $n$, and Averkov, Hojny and Merkert (ICLR 2025) extended the bound to nonbias weights whose denominators are powers of a fixed integer not divisible by a prime $q$. With integer weights the bound is sharp: the classical balanced construction has depth $\lceil\log_2 n\rceil$. For each fixed $q$ and depth cap $K$, we prove this known bound in $\mathbf{VTC}^0$, the bounded-arithmetic theory of uniform constant-depth threshold computation, together with a refuter that is itself such a computation and whose correctness the theory proves. Given a network with at most $K$ hidden layers, nonbias weights whose reduced denominators avoid $q$, arbitrary rational biases and $n>q^K$ inputs, the refuter returns a rational input of polynomial length on which the network errs. The proof makes the obstruction finite: an iterated intersection product of piecewise-linear functions is divisible by $q$ for the network but not for the maximum, so one of finitely many test points exposes the error. By witnessing, provable refutation corresponds to a provably correct refuter, and provable existence of a certified network to a provably correct constructor. On the construction side, we introduce certificates that label each maximum gadget by the inputs it aggregates; with them the theory verifies the balanced construction, which meets the refuter at the sharp threshold $n\le2^K$, and a rational family of polynomial description length and depth $O(\log n/\log\log n)$ built from known depth-two blocks. △ Less

In the framework of the mean field approximation, we numerically solve the corresponding Gross-Pitaevskii equation using the ``Gradient Flow with Discrete Normalization'' method, also known as the imaginary time method. The crystal is artificially produced by applying an external Dirac comb potential to the Bose gas where vacancies are created by randomly removing a predetermined number of deltas. We observe that as the number of randomly removed deltas increases, the ground state energy decreases exponentially from its value for the perfect crystal case until it reaches its value when the Bose gas is free. The ground state energy is reported for different magnitudes of the interaction between bosons and several system sizes which we extrapolate to infinity for the crystal with only one vacancy. Also, we observe the presence of an energy gap between the ground state energies of the perfect system and that with a vacancy, which is more noticeable for values of the particle interaction magnitude $ g \leq 0.1$, when the delta strength $P_0 = 10$. In addition, we report the boson distributions within the crystal, %inside a box with periodic boundary conditions, i.e. the probability density functions which show localization features around vacancies which disappear as $g$ increases. From the ground state energy, the chemical potential is obtained immediately. △ Less

The negative-convexity region expands as the long-run mean of refinancing sensitivity increases. These findings explain negative MBS convexity through the sensitivities of its price components and quantitatively characterize the conditions governing its occurrence. △ Less

We propose a hybrid physics-informed holomorphic neural network (HPIHNN) framework based on a decomposition of the solution into particular and homogeneous components. The particular component is computed by a spectral method on a simple extended domain, while the homogeneous component is approximated by a PIHNN trained only on the boundary with modified boundary data. The results indicate that coupling spectral particular solutions with holomorphic neural approximations provides an efficient approach to nonhomogeneous PDEs on complex domains. △ Less

These schemes are specifically designed to preserve two essential physical properties--concentration bounds and the mass balance identity, and are accompanied by a rigorous error analysis for the projection approach. Both schemes are second-order accurate in space but differ in temporal accuracy: one is first-order and the other is second-order. Methodologically, both schemes comprise a prediction step for the concentration variable, based on a second-order modified upwind discretization together with an implicit-explicit finite difference approximation, followed by a projection (or correction) step that enforces the desired physical constraints, namely bounds and mass conservation. For the first-order temporal scheme, the velocity and pressure are solved at the first step on staggered spatial grids with explicit treatment of concentration. In contrast, for the second-order scheme, to ensure well-posedness and to suppress non-physical oscillations in the simulation, the velocity and pressure are solved at the final step with the concentration treated implicitly. A rigorous numerical theoretical framework is established for the first-order scheme, encompassing optimal-order error estimates, stability, and proofs of the existence and uniqueness of the numerical solutions. The numerical analysis framework is further extended to the more involved second-order scheme, and the corresponding theoretical results are briefly outlined. Finally, numerical examples, including some 'real' simulations, are presented to verify the theoretical findings and demonstrate the strong performance of the proposed schemes. Based on these equivalence results, we formulate a sum-rate maximization problem for the considered multi-user MIMO network and develop an opt… ▽ More Microwave linear analog computer (MiLAC)-aided beamforming, which performs signal processing directly in the analog domain, has recently emerged as a promising alternative to fully digital and hybrid beamforming for multi-antenna systems. However, existing studies have shown that a single lossless and reciprocal MiLAC at the transmitter cannot, in general, attain the performance of fully digital beamforming in multi-user scenarios. To overcome this limitation, we propose a two-layer MiLAC-aided transmit beamforming architecture for downlink multi-user multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) networks. Leveraging multiport microwave network theory, we first establish a general physics-compliant model of the proposed architecture, and then derive a simplified model under the assumptions of unilateral and perfectly matched amplifiers. We prove that, with lossless and reciprocal MiLACs, the two-layer MiLAC-aided transmitter can realize any fully digital transmit beamformer, while a single-layer MiLAC-aided receiver can attain the same achievable rate as a fully digital linear combiner. We then derive closed-form mappings from fully digital transmit beamformers and receive combiners to their corresponding MiLAC-aided analog implementations. Based on these equivalence results, we formulate a sum-rate maximization problem for the considered multi-user MIMO network and develop an optimization framework for jointly designing the power allocation and scattering matrices of the MiLAC-aided transceivers. Numerical results validate the theoretical analysis and show that the proposed two-layer MiLAC-aided design attains the same sum-rate performance as its fully digital counterpart. △ Less

We provide explicit error bounds that quantify the roles of the spectral cutoff, regularization parameter, lengthscale, s… ▽ More Kernel methods face two recurrent bottlenecks: the cubic cost of inverting an $n\times n$ kernel matrix, as in kernel ridge regression, and the evaluation of kernel integrals, as required in kernel quadrature. Existing low-rank kernel and Gaussian process approximations primarily address the former bottleneck, but do not control the downstream quadrature error when both the kernel matrix and kernel mean are approximated, particularly when the integrand lies outside the kernel reproducing kernel Hilbert space. We address both challenges through an operator-adapted finite-feature approximation. Specifically, we construct a finite-dimensional working kernel from the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions of a second-order differential operator. We establish convergence guarantees for the resulting finite-feature estimators in kernel quadrature, with extension to other related kernel methods. Our results show that, with $m \lesssim n$ basis functions, the truncated estimator attains the corresponding benchmark statistical rates. The theory covers fixed/random designs, noisy/noiseless observations, oversmoothing/undersmoothing regimes, and Matérn/Gaussian-type weights. We provide explicit error bounds that quantify the roles of the spectral cutoff, regularization parameter, lengthscale, sampling geometry, and operator boundary condition. We verify the convergence results in numerical experiments. △ Less

    WovenAnchor Matcher: Specialized Intra- and Inter-Image Context Modeling for Feature Matching

    Authors: Zhiyang Li, Ruijiang Jin, Thibaut Klenke, Yusuke Sekikawa, Nakamasa Inoue

    Abstract: Semi-dense local feature matching commonly aggregates contextual information before coarse-to-fine correspondence estimation. However, intra-image and inter-image aggregation serve different roles: the former should propagate spatial evidence within each image while retaining structured two-dimensional dependencies, whereas the latter should gather informati… ▽ More Semi-dense local feature matching commonly aggregates contextual information before coarse-to-fine correspondence estimation. However, intra-image and inter-image aggregation serve different roles: the former should propagate spatial evidence within each image while retaining structured two-dimensional dependencies, whereas the latter should gather information from plausible corresponding regions while limiting noise from unrelated locations. We propose WovenAnchor Matcher (WAM), a semi-dense matching framework based on role-specialized context aggregation. WAM introduces two complementary operators. WovenMamba performs intra-image aggregation through horizontal-then-vertical state-space propagation, allowing vertical updates to operate on horizontally contextualized features and thereby encouraging a structured two-dimensional receptive-field bias. AnchorCrossAttention performs inter-image aggregation by using cross-attention to gather context from plausible corresponding regions across the image pair. It performs this exchange over local representative anchors that aggregate feature evidence within each window. On MegaDepth, WAM achieves 66.1 pose AUC@5$^\circ$, improving on the JamMa baseline by 2.0 AUC points under the same evaluation setup. Ablations show that removing or replacing either specialized component reduces accuracy, providing evidence that intra-image propagation and inter-image retrieval benefit from role-specific aggregation designs. Code and pretrained weights are available at https://github.com/mmaiLab/WovenAnchorMatcher. △ Less

    Regge trajectories from the adjoint sector of Matrix Quantum Mechanics

    Authors: Igor R. Klebanov, Henry W. Lin, Pavel Meshcheriakov

    Abstract: …matrix whose singlet sector is well known to be exactly solvable via free fermions. When the Fermi level approaches a maximum of the potential, there is critical behavior corresponding to string theory in two dimensions. We uncover new phenomena in the adjoint sector by solving the Marchesini-Onofri equation both numerically and analytically using semiclassi… ▽ More We reexamine the large $N$ limit of SU$(N)$ symmetric quantum mechanics of a Hermitian matrix whose singlet sector is well known to be exactly solvable via free fermions. When the Fermi level approaches a maximum of the potential, there is critical behavior corresponding to string theory in two dimensions. We uncover new phenomena in the adjoint sector by solving the Marchesini-Onofri equation both numerically and analytically using semiclassical approximations: at criticality, the spectrum is governed by Regge trajectories with energy eigenvalues growing according to $Δ^2 \sim n/ α'$. In the dual 2D string theory, we interpret these states as oscillatory excitations of a ``short'' folded open string. Up to sub-leading corrections, this Regge behavior is essentially universal and is insensitive to the particular potential we choose to approach criticality. Slightly away from criticality, the highly excited states transition into ``long strings'' that extend far into the Liouville direction. △ Less

    Authors: Somnath Dutta, Koshvendra Singh, Devendra K Ojha, Saurabh Sharma, Tarak Chand, Manojit Chakraborty, Kartik Gokhe, Joe P. Ninan, Jyotirmoy Dey

Assuming optically thin emission and local therm… ▽ More We present near-infrared K-band ($1.94$--$2.52~μ$m) spectroscopy of the deeply embedded protostellar source HOPS\,370, obtained with the TIFR--ARIES Near Infrared Spectrometer (TANSPEC) on the 3.6-m Devasthal Optical Telescope (DOT). Using the intrinsically linked $\rm H_2$ $1$--$0$ Q(3) and $1$--$0$ S(1) transitions, which arise from the same upper energy level, we derive a visual extinction of $A_V = 23.24 \pm 1.25$ mag, corresponding to $A_K = 2.50 \pm 0.13$ mag, consistent with the embedded Class~0/I nature of the source. We further derive an ortho-to-para ratio of $2.45 \pm 0.21$, indicating substantial shock-driven thermal processing while remaining below the high-temperature equilibrium value of 3. Taken together, these diagnostics indicate that the molecular hydrogen emission in HOPS\,370 originates predominantly in warm, shock-heated gas most likely associated with its protostellar outflow. Our results demonstrate the diagnostic power of combined S- and Q-branch $\rm H_2$ spectroscopy for constraining the physical conditions and excitation history of deeply embedded protostellar systems. △ Less

    In-Context Learning for Multi-Fidelity Multiscale Model Closure and Cross-Operator Out-Of-Distribution Generalization

    Authors: Xinyuan Li, Matthew Zahr, Panos Stinis, Zecheng Zhang

    Abstract: …The model is trained using paired low- and high-fidelity examples as demonstrations from multiple operators, together with a new coarse-scale solution query, to predict the corresponding fine-scale solution. Once trained, given a new PDE operator, a few (fewer than five) coarse-fine demonstration pairs, and a low-cost coarse query, the model predicts the… ▽ More Multi-fidelity learning uses low-fidelity information (coarse-scale PDE models) together with limited high-fidelity data (fine-scale solutions) to obtain high-accuracy PDE solutions. It is a data-driven approach to downscale reduced-order models to solve PDEs efficiently and accurately. Standard multi-fidelity learning focuses on one operator or PDE; given a new PDE, retraining using additional coarse-fine solution pairs may be required, which is costly. In this work, we propose an in-context multi-fidelity learning framework for learning multiple multi-fidelity operators with one shared model. The model is trained using paired low- and high-fidelity examples as demonstrations from multiple operators, together with a new coarse-scale solution query, to predict the corresponding fine-scale solution. Once trained, given a new PDE operator, a few (fewer than five) coarse-fine demonstration pairs, and a low-cost coarse query, the model predicts the corresponding fine-scale solution without fine-tuning. When the target operator appears in the training dataset but has a different prediction goal, such as predicting beyond the training time interval, no additional demonstrations are needed. Instead, the model uses training coarse-fine pairs from that PDE and information from other training operators. Testing then only requires generating a low-cost coarse-scale solution query, without fine-tuning. Notably, our smallest model contains only 255,364 trainable parameters, corresponding to approximately 456 parameters per training operator for 560 operators. △ Less

    Criteria for Feasible Monte Carlo Stochastic Simulations of Bosonic Markovian Open Quantum Dynamics

    Authors: Toma Yoneya, Kazuya Fujimoto, Yuki Kawaguchi

    Abstract: …equation, while considering the effects of quantum fluctuations beyond the mean-field approximation. However, the stochastic Monte Carlo simulation is possible only when the corresponding Fokker--Planck equation has a positive-semidefinite diffusion matrix, and the general conditions for the diffusion matrix to be positive semidefinite have remained unclear.… ▽ More The Monte Carlo sampling of the stochastic differential equations (SDEs) based on the quasiprobability distribution function, such as the Glauber--Sudarshan P, Wigner, and Husimi Q functions provides a powerful framework for investigating bosonic open quantum many-body dynamics described by the Gorini--Kossakowski--Sudarshan--Lindblad (GKSL) equation, while considering the effects of quantum fluctuations beyond the mean-field approximation. However, the stochastic Monte Carlo simulation is possible only when the corresponding Fokker--Planck equation has a positive-semidefinite diffusion matrix, and the general conditions for the diffusion matrix to be positive semidefinite have remained unclear. In this work, starting from the path integral formulation, we first derive the sufficient conditions under which the diffusion matrix is positive semidefinite for an arbitrary Hamiltonian, jump operators, and choice of quasiprobability distribution functions. We also analytically derive the corresponding SDEs to be solved. We then investigate the dynamics of the GKSL equation in the thermodynamic limit and show that, depending on the form of the jump operators, the mean-field approximation may fail to describe the dynamics accurately, making stochastic Monte Carlo simulations indispensable. Furthermore, we derive the sufficient conditions under which the higher-order quantum fluctuation terms beyond the Fokker--Planck description vanish identically, even when the jump operators contain quadratic terms. Under these conditions, whenever the corresponding SDEs can be derived, the stochastic Monte Carlo simulation reproduces the exact dynamics. These results clarify the conditions under which the stochastic Monte Carlo simulations are both feasible and necessary for accurately describing the dynamics governed by the GKSL equation in phase space. △ Less

    Exponential decay of threshold correlations for symmetric periodic functions

    Authors: Christina Giannitsi, José Madrid

    Abstract: …. The upper bound in the reciprocal case uses a cyclic covering construction previously studied in the context of circular graphs. We give a self-contained proof of the corresponding optimal modulus. ▽ More We study how often $n$ distinct integer dilates of a periodic function can all lie above a fixed threshold or all lie below its negative. We consider continuous functions $f:\mathbb R\to[-1,1]$ that are $1$-periodic, even, strictly decreasing on $[0,1/2]$, and satisfy $f(0)=1$ and $f(t+1/2)=-f(t)$. This class includes the cosine and the triangle wave. For $0\leqα<1$, let $ρ_α\in(0,1/4]$ be determined by $f(ρ_α)=α$, and let $F_α(n)$ denote the infimum of these proportions over all sets of $n$ distinct positive integer frequencies. We prove that $F_α(n)\geq 4ρ_α^n$ and that the limit $\lim_{n\to\infty}F_α(n)^{1/n}$ exists for every threshold. When $ρ_α^{-1}$ is an integer, we obtain \begin{align} F_α(n) &\leq C_αn^{\log_2((1-ρ_α)/(1-2ρ_α))}ρ_α^n, \end{align} and hence the limit equals $ρ_α$. Otherwise, the limit belongs to the interval $[ρ_α,\lfloorρ_α^{-1}\rfloor^{-1}]$. The upper bound in the reciprocal case uses a cyclic covering construction previously studied in the context of circular graphs. We give a self-contained proof of the corresponding optimal modulus. △ Less

We also determine the largest instantaneous logarithmic-amplitude dissipation function depending only on the graph and the current amplitude, and construct two-level probabilities attaining it.     ScratchWorld: Evaluating If World Models Compute Executable Consequences

    Authors: Yufeng Lin, Jialu Zhang

    Abstract: …attains 91.4% full-state field accuracy on synthetic long-horizon items and 0% changed-field credit on non-empty one-step items, whereas VM replay attains 100% wherever the corresponding metric is defined. Across seven frontier LLMs prompted as world models, state-only $F_1^{\mathrm{VA}}$ reaches at most 30.0% on controlled synthetic mechanisms and declines… ▽ More Evaluations of world models commonly score a predicted future state by its overlap with the ground truth. Yet in interactive worlds, this rewards copying: a model that merely preserves persistent state appears accurate without predicting any consequence of an action. We introduce ScratchWorld, an execution-grounded diagnostic benchmark that treats Scratch projects as executable worlds and derives every label from replay on a pinned Scratch VM, including next-state transitions, hidden variables, causal traces, and counterfactual outcomes. Its primary metric, value-aware changed-field F1 ($F_1^{\mathrm{VA}}$), credits a prediction only when it names a changed field and computes its executed value. Model-free anchors make the confound explicit: a no-change prior attains 91.4% full-state field accuracy on synthetic long-horizon items and 0% changed-field credit on non-empty one-step items, whereas VM replay attains 100% wherever the corresponding metric is defined. Across seven frontier LLMs prompted as world models, state-only $F_1^{\mathrm{VA}}$ reaches at most 30.0% on controlled synthetic mechanisms and declines steeply as projects approach real-world complexity. Revealing the full program raises every model's value-aware score by 22.3-51.7 points and substantially reorders the models, yet the best reaches only 55.4%, an execution gap that persists even with the rules visible. Auxiliary diagnostics complete the error profile: causal-event accuracy remains within 2.5 points of the 50% no-event baseline, with no correct exact chain; counterfactual prediction trades changed-outcome accuracy against invariance; hidden-state accuracy degrades roughly twice as fast as full-state overlap over long horizons. In a word, ScratchWorld separates what a model knows about the rules from what it can actually execute. △ Less

    Sublinear Algorithms for Estimating Single-Linkage Clustering Costs

    Authors: Pan Peng, Christian Sohler, Yi Xu

    Abstract: …connected components. The corresponding \emph{SLC cost profile} is $(\mathrm{cost}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{cost}_n)$, and the scalar quantity $\mathrm{cost}(G)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{cost}_k$ is the area under this profile. We study the problem of approximating these quantities in sublinear time. We assume that the input is a weighted graph $G$ of average degree… ▽ More Single-linkage clustering (SLC) is a fundamental method for hierarchical data analysis. In the distance setting, a $k$-clustering produced by SLC can be obtained by computing a minimum spanning tree (MST) and deleting its $k-1$ heaviest edges. This naturally induces a cost profile for the SLC hierarchy: for each $k\in[n]$, we define $\mathrm{cost}_k$ to be the weight of the resulting $k$-component spanning forest, equivalently, the minimum total weight of any spanning forest with exactly $k$ connected components. The corresponding \emph{SLC cost profile} is $(\mathrm{cost}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{cost}_n)$, and the scalar quantity $\mathrm{cost}(G)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{cost}_k$ is the area under this profile. We study the problem of approximating these quantities in sublinear time. We assume that the input is a weighted graph $G$ of average degree $d$ with edge weights in $\{1,\dots,W\}$, accessed through adjacency-list queries; missing edges are treated as having infinite distance. Our main result is a sampling-based algorithm that outputs a succinct sketch of the entire SLC cost profile in the distance setting. The algorithm runs in $\widetilde{O}(d\sqrt{W}/\varepsilon^3)$ time and returns a sketch from which one can derive estimates $(\widehat{\mathrm{cost}}_1,\ldots,\widehat{\mathrm{cost}}_n)$ satisfying $\sum_{k=1}^{n}\bigl|\widehat{\mathrm{cost}}_k-\mathrm{cost}_k\bigr| \le \varepsilon\,\mathrm{cost}(G)$. Thus, we obtain an $\ell_1$ approximation to the full profile whose error is at most an $\varepsilon$-fraction of the area under the true profile. In particular, this yields a $(1\pm\varepsilon)$-approximation to $\mathrm{cost}(G)$ within the same running time. We also prove a nearly matching lower bound of $Ω(d\sqrt{W}/\varepsilon^2)$ queries for estimating $\mathrm{cost}(G)$. △ Less

    A Differentiable, GPU-Accelerated Finite-Element Formulation in JAX for Large-Deformation Shells with Contact: Application to Patient-Specific Aortic Valve Simulation

    Authors: Mohsen Nakhaei, Alison M. Pouch, Matthew A. Jolley, Wensi Wu

    Abstract: …and one bicuspid) achieved final relative equilibrium residuals below 10^-4. Median GPU runtimes were approximately 15-25 minutes, with 5.3-6.5-fold speedups relative to corresponding CPU runs on the tested hardware. These results provide component-level verification and demonstrate GPU-accelerated forward valve simulation in the tested cases, supporting fut… ▽ More Patient-specific aortic-valve models can help assess how tissue properties and geometry influence leaflet deformation and coaptation, with potential to inform intervention planning. Large deformations, near-incompressibility, and evolving contact make these simulations computationally demanding and can challenge nonlinear convergence. Differentiable, GPU-accelerated methods offer efficient derivative evaluation and parallel computation, but their matrix-free application to soft-tissue shells with contact remains less established. We developed a forward solver using tatva's energy-based finite-element framework in JAX. The solver combines finite-strain extensible-director shells, hyperelastic constitutive models, and a finite-range repulsive contact energy. Nonlinear equilibrium is solved using a regularized, matrix-free Newton-Krylov method with two-level preconditioning. Automatic differentiation evaluates tangent-vector products without forming or storing the primary tangent matrix, while GPU execution parallelizes element and quadrature-point computations. Constitutive, shell, contact, and self-contact responses were assessed against analytical, semi-analytical, and published benchmarks. At a contact-free plate equilibrium, implicit sensitivities of the converged apex displacement to constitutive parameters, thickness, and pressure agreed with finite differences from independent nonlinear simulations. Simulations of three patient-specific aortic-valve models (two tricuspid and one bicuspid) achieved final relative equilibrium residuals below 10^-4. Median GPU runtimes were approximately 15-25 minutes, with 5.3-6.5-fold speedups relative to corresponding CPU runs on the tested hardware. These results provide component-level verification and demonstrate GPU-accelerated forward valve simulation in the tested cases, supporting future gradient-based inverse studies. △ Less

    Recovery of Rough Sources in the Inverse Monge-Ampère Problem

    Authors: Gunther Uhlmann, Philipp Zimmermann

    Abstract: …is locally integrable, Borel measurable, and bounded below by a positive constant. We prove existence of the corresponding Alexandrov solutions and show that the same cylindrical boundary-slope data uniquely determine every nonnegative $L^1$ source. ▽ More We prove that every nonnegative $L^1$ source in the Monge-Ampère equation \[ \det D^2u=f\quad\text{in }Ω, \] on a bounded uniformly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain $Ω\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\geq2$, is uniquely determined by generalized boundary measurements of Alexandrov solutions. The measurement is the extremal boundary slope, which extends the classical outward normal derivative and may take the value $+\infty$. For each direction $ω\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$, it suffices to know these slopes for cylindrical Dirichlet data $\frac{t_k^ω}{2}|P_ωx|^2$ along any sequence $t_k^ω\to\infty$, where $P_ω$ projects onto $ω^\perp$. The boundary value and extremal slope determine the interior subgradient image up to a Lebesgue null set. An exact change of variables formula then converts equality of these images into integral identities for the source, and a large-amplitude limit recovers its Euclidean X-ray transform. This yields uniqueness without a positive lower bound on the source. For nonnegative $L^\infty$ sources, the extremal slopes are bounded and agree with the interior normal derivatives, giving a Dirichlet-to-interior-Neumann formulation. We also extend the inverse argument to the gradient-weighted equation \[ \vartheta(\nabla u)\det D^2u=f, \] where the known weight $\vartheta$ is locally integrable, Borel measurable, and bounded below by a positive constant. We prove existence of the corresponding Alexandrov solutions and show that the same cylindrical boundary-slope data uniquely determine every nonnegative $L^1$ source. △ Less

    Dissipative Dynamical Phase Transition as a Complex Ising Model

    Authors: Stephen W. Yan, Diego Barberena, Matthew P. A. Fisher, Sagar Vijay

    Abstract: …observables analytically and uncover a dynamical phase transition as the relative rate of unitary evolution and dissipation is tuned. We show that the weak-dissipation limit corresponds to a cTFIM with a large magnitude of the imaginary transverse-field, for which the many-body "ground-state" (with smallest real eigenvalue) is gapless, exhibiting qua… ▽ More We investigate a quantum dynamical phase transition induced by the competition between local unitary evolution and dissipation in a qubit chain with a strong, on-site $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry. While the steady-state of this evolution is always maximally-mixed, we show that the dynamical behavior of certain non-local observables on the approach to this steady-state is dictated by a quantum Ising model with a complex transverse-field (cTFIM). A crucial feature of our mapping to the cTFIM is an extensive number of local, weak, $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetries possessed by the dynamics, allowing us to investigate these observables analytically and uncover a dynamical phase transition as the relative rate of unitary evolution and dissipation is tuned. We show that the weak-dissipation limit corresponds to a cTFIM with a large magnitude of the imaginary transverse-field, for which the many-body "ground-state" (with smallest real eigenvalue) is gapless, exhibiting quasi-long-range correlations of the local magnetization with a continuously-varying exponent. Correspondingly, the dynamics of the non-local observables show oscillatory behavior with an amplitude decaying exponentially in time. The strong-dissipation limit corresponds to a gapped ferromagnetic phase of the cTFIM, and non-local observables show exponential decay on the approach to equilibrium. This transition in (1+1)-dimensions has a peculiar, "two-sided" nature appearing as either first- or second-order depending on the phase from which the transition is approached, an analytic result which is corroborated by numerical studies. In higher dimensions, we present a field-theoretic understanding of the first-order nature of this transition, when approaching from the ferromagnetic phase of the cTFIM, though the nature of the phase with large imaginary transverse-field remains to be understood. △ Less

    Higher-order Approximate Symmetries

    Authors: Alexey Shevyakov

    Abstract: …on a triangular system for the coefficients of a solution expansion, is extended to arbitrary order. The two approaches are compared for a perturbed cubic wave equation. Nonlinearities admitting an FS point symmetry with pr… ▽ More The Fushchych--Shtelen (FS) construction of approximate symmetries, based on a triangular system for the coefficients of a solution expansion, is extended to arbitrary order. A coefficient-map formulation establishes, under explicit hypotheses, a correspondence with the Baikov--Gazizov--Ibragimov (BGI) method. The two approaches are compared for a perturbed cubic wave equation. Nonlinearities admitting an FS point symmetry with prescribed projection onto the unperturbed dilation are classified through second order, up to equivalences. They comprise a power family and logarithmic families at exceptional exponents. Nonlinearities admitting second-order BGI point symmetries that continue this dilation are also classified. Among perturbations nontrivial at first order, these form a proper subfamily of one FS family; another family is admitted by both methods when the perturbation first enters at second order. Joint reduction by the Lorentz algebra and the inherited FS dilation yields explicit invariant solutions for all FS normal forms and recovers the first-order solutions of Fushchych and Shtelen. For the common family with a nontrivial first-order perturbation, the logarithmic corrections arise from expanding a power law with a perturbation-dependent exponent. Periodic traveling waves illustrate the long-scale validity of the FS expansion. The first correction is obtained by quadrature, second-order secular terms are identified, and phase renormalization yields bounded approximations. For integer-power members of the power family, the amplitude dependence of the corrected wavenumber agrees with the symmetry scaling weights. △ Less

The scheme is based on a continuous pseudostress-velocity augmented dual-mixed finite element formulation, in which the Dirichlet datum is imposed naturally, whereas the prescribed normal trace of the pseudostress is incorporated into the $H(div,)$ stress space.     Sample Complexity for Generative Learning of Quantum Measurement Data

    Authors: Shuhan Zhang, Yi-Zhuang You

    Abstract: …. Bulk-site results are consistent with the corresponding finite-sample predictions. These results connect the structure of quantum measurement distributions to the model capacity justified by finite data. ▽ More Generative learning of a quantum measurement distribution from finite data requires choosing a model capacity that balances representation accuracy against estimation cost. We develop a bias-variance framework that decomposes the expected population Kullback-Leibler error at model capacity $s$ and sample size $N$ as $\mathcal E(s,N)=B(s)+V(s,N)$, with representation bias $B$ and a variance term $V$ capturing the mean estimation error. In the well-sampled regime, $V(s,N)\simeq[NC(s)]^{-1}$, and exponential or algebraic laws for $B$ and $C$ yield four scaling templates for the optimal error, from inverse powers of $N$ to inverse powers of $\ln N$. For local autoregressive models, the context radius sets the model capacity, and $B$ is the summed conditional mutual information lost by truncating the context. For a smoothed histogram learner, we derive an exact finite-sample estimation cost that accounts for rarely observed contexts and recovers the parameter-count law in the well-sampled regime. For transverse-field Ising Born distributions, the independently evaluated bias and estimation cost predict both the optimal context radius and the optimal error. In the well-sampled regime, the estimation cost grows exponentially with context radius. Its competition with approximately exponential bias decay in the gapped regimes predicts power-law error decay in $N$, while algebraic bias decay at criticality predicts asymptotic inverse-power decay in $\ln N$. Bulk-site results are consistent with the corresponding finite-sample predictions. These results connect the structure of quantum measurement distributions to the model capacity justified by finite data. △ Less

    An Improved Johnson Bound for List-Decoding with Hints

    Authors: Swastik Kopparty, Harry Sha

    Abstract: This paper studies when list-decodability guarantees for error-correcting codes of good distance can go beyond the classical Johnson radius and gives a corresponding efficient list-decoding algorithm for Reed-Solomon codes up to this new radius. The Johnson bound shows that any error-correcting code with distance $δ$ is automatically list-decodable with smal… ▽ More This paper studies when list-decodability guarantees for error-correcting codes of good distance can go beyond the classical Johnson radius and gives a corresponding efficient list-decoding algorithm for Reed-Solomon codes up to this new radius. The Johnson bound shows that any error-correcting code with distance $δ$ is automatically list-decodable with small list size up to a certain radius $J(δ)$ around any received word $r$. We show that if the received word $r$ is known to have some codewords that are somewhat close to it (we call these ``hints"), then $C$ is list-decodable with small list size up to an even larger radius around $r$. We determine the optimal list-decoding radius in this setting for single and multiple hints over both large and small alphabets and show that this radius is the optimum of a natural convex program. Remarkably, even a single hint that is $(J(δ)-ε)$-close to $r$ improves the list-decoding radius around $r$ to $J(δ)+ε'$. Finally, we give an efficient algorithm, based on the weighted Guruswami-Sudan decoder, that list-decodes Reed-Solomon codes up to this optimal radius. △ Less

This work investigates whether early adaptation can be accelerated using an initialization learned from a collection of previously solved registration tasks.
